My cat, Mr. Whiskers—he was this huge orange tabby—he was in the room when I did it and he jumped so high he knocked over a stack of floppy disks. Disks everywhere. Took me an hour to sort them back into their cases. \n\nBut, uh... yeah. Anyway, that washed-out feeling. That's pretty much how I've been feeling most of the time lately. Just kind of sad and heavy.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, sad feeling most days?\n\nPatient: Well, looking back... I'd say these low, guilty periods have been hanging around, off and on, for about eleven months now. Almost a year. \n\nIt started right around the time the leaves were changing last fall. We have this huge oak tree in our front yard, and the acorns were just dropping like crazy. It sounded like hail on the roof. I remember because I had to get up on the ladder to clean out the gutters, and I hate heights. My neighbor, Dave, he's got one of those fancy gutter-guard systems installed. He was out there just watching me scoop out this black, rotting leaf mush with a plastic trowel. Dave's a nice enough guy, but he's always bragging about his lawn. He uses this special fertilizer he orders from some farm co-op in the Midwest. It smells awful for about three days, but I have to admit, his grass is incredibly green. He actually won yard of the month from the HOA back in July. They give you this little wooden sign to put in your yard, but the paint on it is chipping, so it doesn't even look that great...\n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, about eleven months of feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies, activities, or even spending time with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, I have. Like, I usually look forward to grilling on the weekend, or going fishing, or even just putting on a record, but lately... I just leave all that stuff sitting there and I don't really care. \n\nLike my grill, it's this really nice Weber charcoal one, not the gas kind. I always thought gas grills were basically just outdoor stoves, you know? You don't get that real smoky flavor. Anyway, I bought this special mesquite wood chunks for it a while back. I had to order them online because the hardware store only had hickory, and hickory can be a little overpowering if you're doing chicken. The shipping took forever, though. The tracking said it was stuck in a facility in Memphis for like four days. I don't know what it is about Memphis, but every time I order something and it goes through there, it just sits. My buddy Greg used to live in Memphis. He worked for a logistics company, ironically enough. He said the barbecue there is incredible, but the humidity in the summer is just brutal. He ended up moving to Denver just to escape the heat. Denver's nice, but the altitude really messes with my ears... \n\nUm, but yeah. I just haven't had the energy or interest to even uncover the grill lately. It's just sitting there on the patio. Same with my records and fishing gear. Just don't care right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or having trouble getting your energy up, even when you're not doing anything physically demanding?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. It's like... by the time I get home from the office, I'm just totally drained. Even simple things like checking the mail or loading the dishwasher feel like way more effort than they should. \n\nSpeaking of the dishwasher, it's been making this weird grinding noise lately during the rinse cycle. I think a piece of glass or something might be stuck in the filter at the bottom. I watched a YouTube video on how to take it apart, and the guy in the video had the exact same model, but his kitchen was completely remodeled. Like, granite countertops, subway tile backsplash, the works. It made me realize how outdated our kitchen is. The cabinets are that old honey oak color from the 90s, and the hardware is all brass. My wife has been talking about painting them white, but I read online that if you don't prep them right, the paint just chips off in a few months. You have to use this special deglosser, and then a really good primer, like Kilz or something. My dad used to swear by Kilz. He used it when he painted the garage floor back at the old house. He put down those little colored flakes, too, so it looked like a showroom floor... \n\nBut, uh, anyway. Yeah, I just don't have the energy for anything. Just getting through the day takes it all out of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately, like eating less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually. My appetite has been pretty off lately. I've mostly just been grabbing a few bites here and there, or completely skipping supper altogether. And I noticed my belt is a little looser, even though I'm definitely not trying to lose weight or anything. \n\nIt's funny you mention belts, because I've had the same brown leather belt for like, I don't know, maybe seven years? I got it at this little leather shop when we took a road trip down to the Smokies. The guy there, he made them all by hand. He had this massive workbench covered in all these specialized tools—awls and hole punches and these little mallets. He said he used to work in a factory making car upholstery before he opened the shop. Car upholstery is fascinating when you think about it. The stitching has to be so precise, especially on leather seats, or else it just pulls apart when it gets hot in the summer. My first car was a Honda Civic, and the cloth seats in that thing faded so badly after just a couple of years. It was parked in the sun at the high school every day, so I guess that makes sense. I tried putting those cheap seat covers on it, the ones with the elastic bands that hook under the seat, but they always slid around whenever you took a sharp turn...\n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, my belt. Yeah, it's definitely looser, and I'm just not eating much at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worse about yourself lately—like you're letting people down or that you're not as good as others seem to think you are?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. That's actually a big part of why I came in. I just keep beating myself up lately, thinking I'm not really senior material at work, you know? Like I somehow fooled everyone into thinking I could handle more than I actually can. \n\nIt's funny, because when I first started as a systems analyst, I was so eager to learn everything. I remember setting up my first server rack, and I was so meticulous with the cable management. I used these specific Velcro ties instead of zip ties. Zip ties are the worst in a server room because if you ever have to replace a single cable, you have to snip the whole bundle and risk cutting a wire. I learned that the hard way when I accidentally snipped a fiber optic cable at my last job. Fiber is so fragile, not like copper Ethernet. And the splicing kits for fiber are ridiculously expensive. We had to call in this outside contractor to fix it, and this guy showed up in a van that looked like a mobile laboratory. He had this fusion splicer machine that uses an electric arc to melt the glass together. It was actually incredible to watch. He said he used to do underwater welding before he got into fiber optics. I can't even imagine being underwater in one of those heavy suits, just surrounded by the ocean. I've always been a little claustrophobic, so scuba diving was never really on my bucket list. My wife went snorkeling in Hawaii once and said she saw a sea turtle, but she also got a terrible sunburn on the back of her legs because she forgot to reapply sunscreen...\n\nBut, um, yeah. The work stuff. I just feel like a fraud lately, like I'm letting my team down and I'm just not as competent as they all think I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, my wife hasn't said I'm moving like a turtle or bouncing off the walls or anything. \n\nSpeaking of turtles, I know I just mentioned the one my wife saw in Hawaii, but it reminded me of this turtle we had when I was a kid. It was a red-eared slider, and we kept it in this big glass tank in the living room. My mom hated cleaning that tank. You have to have a really good filtration system for turtles because they produce a lot of waste. We had this canister filter that sat under the stand, and you had to change the charcoal in it every few weeks. Charcoal is amazing stuff, really. I use a charcoal chimney starter when I grill out back. It's so much better than lighter fluid. Lighter fluid just makes all the food taste like chemicals. I usually grill burgers or maybe some bratwursts. We get the brats from this local butcher shop down the street. The guy who runs it is a third-generation butcher. His grandfather came over from Germany and started the shop back in the 50s. They still use the original meat grinder, this huge cast-iron thing. Cast iron is great if you season it right. I have a skillet that I use for everything, but you can't ever wash it with soap, or it ruins the seasoning... \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Moving slow or being restless. No, I haven't really noticed any of that. Just the being tired part.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying, or wished you were dead, even just briefly?\n\nPatient: Well... I haven't made any actual plans to hurt myself or anything like that. But I do catch myself thinking, you know, it might just be easier if I didn't wake up some morning. \n\nWaking up has just been tough. I actually still use this old alarm clock I bought back in high school. It's one of those clunky ones with the bright red LED numbers. I hate using my phone as an alarm because if I leave it on the nightstand, I always end up looking at work emails before I even get out of bed. And email is just a nightmare right now. We migrated our whole company to a new exchange server last month, and the spam filters are completely out of whack. People are getting legitimate client emails sent straight to their junk folders. Spam filters are actually pretty complex algorithms if you look at how they weigh different keywords and sender reputations. It's kind of like how search engines rank websites. Back in the early days of the internet, people used to just stuff the bottom of their web pages with invisible text, like white text on a white background, just to trick the search engines into ranking them higher. You can't get away with that anymore, obviously. Google's web crawlers are way too smart for that now. They actually penalize your site if they catch you doing it, and it can take months to get your domain reputation back in good standing... \n\nBut, um, sorry, what was I saying? Oh, right. Not waking up. Yeah, I don't have a plan to do anything bad, just... sometimes I think it would be easier to just sleep and not have to deal with everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. My wife hasn't said I'm moving like a turtle or bouncing off the walls or anything. \n\nSpeaking of turtles, I know I just mentioned the one my wife saw in Hawaii, but it reminded me of this turtle we had when I was a kid. Or maybe I'm getting my conversations mixed up. But no, like I said, I haven't been moving in slow motion or bouncing off the walls or anything. I don't feel restless or jittery. \n\nThough speaking of jittery, I actually had to switch to decaf in the afternoons recently. I used to drink this really strong cold brew from a coffee shop near my office. Cold brew is totally different from regular iced coffee, you know. They steep the grounds in cold water for like twenty-four hours, so it extracts all the caffeine but leaves behind the acidity. It's really smooth. But the filtration process can be a huge pain if you try to make it at home. I tried making it myself using this fine mesh sieve, but the grounds always got stuck in the little holes. Mesh networks are actually kind of similar when you think about it. We use mesh Wi-Fi at work to cover the dead zones in the warehouse. Instead of one router, you have all these nodes that talk to each other to blanket the area. It's way more efficient for data packet routing. Data packets can get dropped so easily if the network topology isn't set up right. I had to reconfigure our entire office layout last year because the previous IT guy just daisy-chained a bunch of cheap switches together. You get terrible latency doing it that way. Latency is the absolute worst when you're trying to remote into a server and your mouse cursor takes a full second to catch up to your hand... \n\nUm, anyway, sorry. What was the question again? Not like I can't do my job, but my mind just kind of drifts off. \n\nIt actually happened a lot yesterday when I was trying to compile this report for our quarterly review. Quarterly reviews are such a headache. Our CFO always wants these specific metrics pulled from the database, but the way our legacy system stores the data makes it incredibly tedious to extract. We're still running on this ancient SQL server from like 2012. I've been telling management we need to migrate to the cloud for years. Cloud computing is just so much more scalable, especially with AWS or Azure. Azure is Microsoft, obviously, and they've really cornered the enterprise market. Bill Gates really knew what he was doing when he pivoted the company toward enterprise solutions. Did you know he bought the original DOS operating system from another developer for like fifty grand and then licensed it to IBM? It's probably the greatest business deal in history. IBM didn't think the software was where the money was; they just cared about the hardware. Hardware is getting so expensive now, though, especially with the global chip shortage. I tried to buy a new graphics card for my home PC last month, and the prices are just insane because of all the crypto miners buying them up. It's basically a decentralized public ledger, which means it's incredibly secure because every node has to verify the transaction...\n\nBut, um, wait. What were you asking me? Oh, right. Concentrating. Yeah, it's just been a bit tough to stay focused on one thing for very long.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down or that it's harder to think clearly lately?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say my thoughts are slowed down. If anything, I feel like I'm thinking perfectly clearly, just... maybe about too many things at once. But not slow.\n\nSpeaking of slow, though, the traffic on the way over here was absolutely brutal. They've got the interstate down to one lane for construction, and of course, nobody knows how to zipper merge. It's mathematically proven that the zipper merge is the most efficient way to handle a lane closure, but people get so territorial about their spot in line. It's basic game theory, really. People act in their own short-term self-interest instead of cooperating for the optimal outcome. Game theory is fascinating. John Nash won a Nobel Prize for his work on non-cooperative games. Have you ever seen *A Beautiful Mind*? Russell Crowe was great in that, though they definitely took some liberties with the actual mathematics he was doing. Hollywood always does that. They try to make math look visual and exciting, like floating numbers in the air. Real math is mostly just staring at a whiteboard for hours. I used to do a lot of whiteboard diagramming when I was mapping out network architectures. You need a good set of dry-erase markers, though. The cheap ones always leave that ghosting on the board, and you end up having to use that special spray cleaner. That spray smells exactly like rubbing alcohol. We used to use rubbing alcohol to clean the tape heads on our old VCR when I was a kid. You had to use a cotton swab and be really gentle so you didn't knock the heads out of alignment...\n\nSorry, what was the question? Oh, thinking clearly. Yeah, my thoughts aren't slow at all. I can think just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that nothing gives you pleasure anymore, not even things you used to really enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose that's true. I usually look forward to grilling on the weekend, or heading out fishing, or even just putting on a record in the evening. But lately... I don't know, I just leave the stuff sitting there. I just don't really care. \n\nMy turntable has just been gathering dust, actually. It's a vintage direct-drive Technics. Direct-drive is so much better than belt-drive for pitch stability because the motor is connected right to the platter. DJs always used them back in the day. The audio fidelity on a good vinyl setup is just so much warmer than digital. It's because it's a continuous analog waveform. Digital audio has to chop the sound wave into discrete steps, usually at 44.1 kilohertz if we're talking CD quality. That specific number is based on the Nyquist-Shannon sampling theorem—it basically says you have to sample at twice the highest frequency of human hearing to accurately reproduce the sound. It's really elegant math. Claude Shannon basically invented information theory when he was at Bell Labs. Bell Labs was incredible, really the absolute peak of American innovation. They invented the transistor there. Without the transistor, modern computing literally wouldn't exist. We'd still be using giant vacuum tubes that take up entire rooms and burn out every few days. My grandfather had this huge old tube radio, and it took a solid five minutes just to warm up before you could tune into a station...\n\nUm, anyway. Sorry. I lost track again. What did you ask? Oh, right, pleasure and enjoying things. Yeah, like I said, the grill, the fishing rods, the records... they're just sitting there. I just haven't cared enough to use them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm moving slow. And I'm not really restless, either. I'm just kind of... normal, I guess. I sit at my desk most of the day anyway, so it's not like I'm moving around a ton to begin with. \n\nMy desk chair is actually starting to give out on me, though. The pneumatic cylinder keeps sinking. I'll be typing away, and over the course of an hour, I slowly drop like three inches until my knees are practically hitting the underside of the desk. You can actually replace just the cylinder instead of buying a whole new chair, but getting the old one out of the wheelbase is a nightmare. You usually have to hit it with a rubber mallet, and even then, they get so seized up with rust and dust over the years. WD-40 doesn't even touch it. People think WD-40 is a lubricant, but it's actually a water displacement formula—that's what the WD stands for. It was the 40th attempt at the formula by this company back in the 50s. They were trying to create a rust-preventative solvent and degreaser to protect the outer skin of the Atlas missile. The Atlas missile program was huge during the Cold War. It was the first operational intercontinental ballistic missile the US had. The engineering that went into those things was incredible, especially considering they were doing all the calculations on slide rules. Slide rules are actually fascinating. It's basically a mechanical analog computer that uses logarithms to do multiplication and division. My dad had one from when he was in engineering school, and he tried to teach me how to use it once, but it's so counterintuitive compared to just punching numbers into a calculator or a spreadsheet...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? What was the question? Oh, moving slow or being restless. No, neither of those. I've just been moving normally."
